I have always been a very active, engaged, and organized person, so this constant heavy feeling is entirely new to me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling this way for months; has this heavy, sad feeling been present almost every single day for at least the past two years, or is this a more recent change that started within the last few months?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been two years—it started about eight months ago. I haven't noticed any major changes in my appetite, though.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you experienced any feelings of worthlessness or excessive, inappropriate guilt, or have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel like I'm failing everyone—like I'm a lousy sister and a poor friend who doesn't bring much to anyone's life anymore. On my lowest days, I do sometimes think that if I just didn't wake up it would be a relief for me and make things simpler for everyone, though I don't have any plans to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of worthlessness and your lack of interest in activities become so severe that you have completely stopped functioning in your daily responsibilities, or are you still able to manage your basic tasks despite these feelings?\n\nPatient: I haven't completely stopped functioning, but my daily responsibilities have become significantly harder to manage because I feel so low and drained. I've let my intergenerational art circle stall out and find myself frequently skipping or canceling social plans with my friends. I'm also really struggling to keep up with coordinating my sister's medical appointments and paperwork, which used to be much easier for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you have significant difficulty concentrating or making decisions on most days?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any slowing down in my speech or movements. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. For months now I’ve had this constant heavy, sad feeling that I just can’t seem to shake, even when I’m doing things I used to enjoy. It has just been hanging over me, and I finally realized I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced similar periods of feeling this way in the past, or is this the first time you have felt like this?\n\nPatient: This is actually the very first time I have ever felt this way. I have always been a very active, engaged, and organized person, so this constant heavy feeling is entirely new to me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling this way for months; has this heavy, sad feeling been present almost every single day for at least the past two years, or is this a more recent change that started within the last few months?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been two years—it started about eight months ago. It began around the time my sister was diagnosed with a chronic heart condition, and I took on the responsibility of managing her care from a distance. Since then, this heavy feeling has just been there almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Since this started eight months ago, have you noticed any significant changes in your sleep patterns, appetite, or your energy levels throughout the day?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been quite disrupted; I fall asleep fine but wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with my mind racing about my sister, leaving me feeling constantly tired and drained. I haven't noticed any major changes in my appetite, though.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you experienced any feelings of worthlessness or excessive, inappropriate guilt, or have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel like I'm failing everyone—like I'm a lousy sister and a poor friend who doesn't bring much to anyone's life anymore. On my lowest days, I do sometimes think that if I just didn't wake up it would be a relief for me and make things simpler for everyone, though I don't have any plans to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of worthlessness and your lack of interest in activities become so severe that you have completely stopped functioning in your daily responsibilities, or are you still able to manage your basic tasks despite these feelings?\n\nPatient: I haven't completely stopped functioning, but my daily responsibilities have become significantly harder to manage because I feel so low and drained. I've let my intergenerational art circle stall out and find myself frequently skipping or canceling social plans with my friends. I'm also really struggling to keep up with coordinating my sister's medical appointments and paperwork, which used to be much easier for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you have significant difficulty concentrating or making decisions on most days?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any slowing down in my speech or movements. My concentration and ability to make decisions seem fine too, despite how tired I feel.\n\nDoctor: Instead of feeling slowed down, do you find yourself feeling physically restless, like you are unable to sit still or need to pace around because of your internal tension?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel physically restless or have any urge to pace at all. I don't experience that kind of physical tension; instead, I just feel very physically drained, heavy, and tired.\n\nDoctor: If the stress related to your sister's illness were to be resolved or significantly reduced, do you feel that your mood and symptoms would likely improve?"

My concentration and ability to make decisions seem fine too, despite how tired I feel.\n\nDoctor: Instead of feeling slowed down, do you find yourself feeling physically restless, like you are unable to sit still or need to pace around because of your internal tension?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel physically restless or have any urge to pace at all. I don't experience that kind of physical tension; instead, I just feel very physically drained, heavy, and tired.\n\nDoctor: If the stress related to your sister's illness were to be resolved or significantly reduced, do you feel that your mood and symptoms would likely improve?\n\nPatient: While the stress with my sister definitely started this downward spiral, I'm not entirely sure if resolving it would completely lift this heavy feeling now. This sadness and exhaustion feel so constant and deeply set in, like a gray film over everything, that it is hard to imagine just bouncing back even if the caregiving stress went away.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the stress related to your sister's care, do you find yourself feeling this deep sadness and lack of interest in things you used to enjoy even during moments when you are not actively thinking about her or her health?"
  }

Yes, absolutely. Even when I am at my watercolor group or walking club and trying to focus on other things, that heavy gray film is still there and I just go through the motions. The sadness and lack of interest remain constant, regardless of whether I'm actively thinking about my sister or not.

I haven't noticed any major changes in my appetite, though.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you experienced any feelings of worthlessness or excessive, inappropriate guilt, or have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel like I'm failing everyone—like I'm a lousy sister and a poor friend who doesn't bring much to anyone's life anymore. On my lowest days, I do sometimes think that if I just didn't wake up it would be a relief for me and make things simpler for everyone, though I don't have any plans to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of worthlessness and your lack of interest in activities become so severe that you have completely stopped functioning in your daily responsibilities, or are you still able to manage your basic tasks despite these feelings?\n\nPatient: I haven't completely stopped functioning, but my daily responsibilities have become significantly harder to manage because I feel so low and drained. I've let my intergenerational art circle stall out and find myself frequently skipping or canceling social plans with my friends. I'm also really struggling to keep up with coordinating my sister's medical appointments and paperwork, which used to be much easier for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you have significant difficulty concentrating or making decisions on most days?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any slowing down in my speech or movements. My concentration and ability to make decisions seem fine too, despite how tired I feel.\n\nDoctor: Instead of feeling slowed down, do you find yourself feeling physically restless, like you are unable to sit still or need to pace around because of your internal tension?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel physically restless or have any urge to pace at all. I don't experience that kind of physical tension; instead, I just feel very physically drained, heavy, and tired.\n\nDoctor: If the stress related to your sister's illness were to be resolved or significantly reduced, do you feel that your mood and symptoms would likely improve?\n\nPatient: While the stress with my sister definitely started this downward spiral, I'm not entirely sure if resolving it would completely lift this heavy feeling now. This sadness and exhaustion feel so constant and deeply set in, like a gray film over everything, that it is hard to imagine just bouncing back even if the caregiving stress went away.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the stress related to your sister's care, do you find yourself feeling this deep sadness and lack of interest in things you used to enjoy even during moments when you are not actively thinking about her or her health?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Even when I am at my watercolor group or walking club and trying to focus on other things, that heavy gray film is still there and I just go through the motions. The sadness and lack of interest remain constant, regardless of whether I'm actively thinking about my sister or not.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your current symptoms, such as the persistent sadness and loss of interest, are primarily a direct emotional reaction to the specific stress of your sister's illness, or do they feel like a separate, pervasive condition that has taken on a life of its own?\n\nPatient: Although these feelings definitely started when my sister fell ill and I took on her care eight months ago, they now feel like they have taken on a life of their own. This heavy, sad feeling is constant and sits with me all day long, even when I'm not actively dealing with her care. It really feels more like a pervasive cloud that is always there, rather than just a temporary reaction to the stress."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nThis is actually the very first time I have ever felt this way.
